[college_physics] Q1  answer=D
A photon strikes an electron of mass m that is initially at rest, creating an electron-positron pair. The photon is destroyed and the positron and two electrons move off at equal speeds along the initial direction of the photon. The energy of the photon was
A. mc^2
B. 2mc^2
C. 3mc^2
D. 4mc^2
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given that a photon strikes an electron at rest, and the result is the creation of an electron-positron pair. The photon is destroyed, and the final products are:

- The original electron,
- A newly created positron,
- And a newly created electron,

So in total, there are now **two electrons and one positron** moving off at equal speeds along the initial direction of the photon.

But wait — the initial system has:
- A photon (energy E, momentum p = E/c),
- One electron (mass m, at rest).

After the interaction:
- Three particles: two electrons and one positron, each with mass m (since electron and positron have same mass),
- All moving at equal speeds along the photon’s initial direction.

Let’s denote the final speed of each particle as v. Since all three particles have the same speed and are moving in the same direction, their momenta are all in the same direction.

Let’s analyze conservation of energy and momentum.

---

### Step 1: Conservation of Momentum

Initial momentum: only from photon:  
p_initial = E / c

Final momentum: total momentum of three particles, each with relativistic momentum:

Each particle has relativistic momentum:  
p = γmv  
where γ = 1 / √(1 - v²/c²)

Since all three particles are identical in mass and speed and direction, total final momentum:

p_final = 3γmv

Conservation:  
E / c = 3γmv  
⇒ E = 3γmvc  — (1)

---

### Step 2: Conservation of Energy

Initial energy:
- Photon: E
- Electron: rest energy = mc²

Total initial energy: E + mc²

Final energy: energy of three particles (each with relativistic energy γmc²)

Total final energy: 3γmc²

So:
E + mc² = 3γmc²  
⇒ E = 3γmc² - mc² = mc²(3γ - 1) — (2)

---

Now we have two expressions for E:

From (1): E = 3γmvc  
From (2): E = mc²(3γ - 1)

Set them equal:

3γmvc = mc²(3γ - 1)

Divide both sides by m:

3γvc = c²(3γ - 1)

Divide both sides by c:

3γv = c(3γ - 1)

Now solve for v:

3γv = 3γc - c  
⇒ 3γv - 3γc = -c  
⇒ 3γ(v - c) = -c  
⇒ γ(v - c) = -c/3

But better to rearrange:

3γv = 3γc - c  
⇒ 3γv = c(3γ - 1)  
⇒ v = [c(3γ - 1)] / (3γ) = c(1 - 1/(3γ))

Now recall that γ = 1 / √(1 - v²/c²)

Let’s substitute.

Set β = v/c, so γ = 1 / √(1 - β²)

From above:  
v = c(1 - 1/(3γ))  
⇒ β = 1 - 1/(3γ)

Now substitute into γ:

γ = 1 / √(1 - β²)  
But β = 1 - 1/(3γ)

This is messy, but let’s try to solve numerically.

Let’s suppose that γ is large, but let’s plug in values.

Alternatively, suppose that the photon energy is such that the final particles are relativistic.

But let’s try to use the fact that the total final energy is 3γmc², and the initial energy is E + mc², so:

E = 3γmc² - mc²

And E = 3γmvc

So:

3γmvc = 3γmc² - mc²

Divide both sides by mc:

3γv = 3γc - c  
⇒ 3γ(v - c) = -c  
⇒ γ(v - c) = -c/3  
⇒ v = c - c/(3γ) = c(1 - 1/(3γ))

Now, since γ = 1 / √(1 - v²/c²), substitute v:

Let’s write:

v = c(1 - 1/(3γ))

So β = 1 - 1/(3γ)

Now compute γ:

γ = 1 / √(1 - β²) = 1 / √(1 - (1 - 1/(3γ))²)

Compute (1 - 1/(3γ))² = 1 - 2/(3γ) + 1/(9γ²)

So:

1 - β² = 1 - [1 - 2/(3γ) + 1/(9γ²)] = 2/(3γ) - 1/(9γ²)

So:

γ = 1 / √(2/(3γ) - 1/(9γ²))

Let’s set x = γ, so:

x = 1 / √(2/(3x) - 1/(9x²))  
= 1 / √( (6x - 1)/(9x²) )  
= 1 / [ √(6x - 1) / (3x) ]  
= 3x / √(6x - 1)

So:

x = 3x / √(6x - 1)

Divide both sides by x (assuming x ≠ 0):

1 = 3 / √(6x - 1)

⇒ √(6x - 1) = 3  
⇒ 6x - 1 = 9  
⇒ 6x = 10  
⇒ x = 10/6 = 5/3

So γ = 5/3

Now, back to energy:

From (2): E = mc²(3γ - 1) = mc²(3*(5/3) - 1) = mc²(5 - 1) = 4mc²

So the energy of the photon is 4mc².

Answer: D
→ truth=D  predicted=D  correct=True  tokens=1337  latency=9.6s

[college_physics] Q2  answer=A
Protons used in cancer therapy are typically accelerated to about 0.6c . How much work must be done on a particle of mass m in order for it to reach this speed, assuming it starts at rest?
A. 0.25mc^2
B. 0.60mc^2
C. 0.67mc^2
D. 1.25mc^2
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To find the work required to accelerate a particle of mass \( m \) to speed \( v = 0.6c \), we need to calculate the relativistic kinetic energy, since the speed is a significant fraction of the speed of light.

The relativistic kinetic energy is given by:

\[
K = (\gamma - 1)mc^2
\]

where

\[
\gamma = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - v^2/c^2}}
\]

Given \( v = 0.6c \), we compute:

\[
\gamma = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - (0.6)^2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - 0.36}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{0.64}} = \frac{1}{0.8} = 1.25
\]

Then:

\[
K = (1.25 - 1)mc^2 = 0.25mc^2
\]

So, the work required is \( 0.25mc^2 \).

Answer: A

[college_physics] Q3  answer=D
A resistor in a circuit dissipates energy at a rate of 1 W. If the voltage across the resistor is doubled, what will be the new rate of energy dissipation?
A. 0.25 W
B. 0.5 W
C. 1 W
D. 4 W
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The power dissipated by a resistor is given by the formula:

P = V² / R

Where:
- P is power (in watts),
- V is voltage (in volts),
- R is resistance (in ohms).

Initially, P = 1 W.

If the voltage is doubled (new voltage = 2V), then the new power is:

P_new = (2V)² / R = 4V² / R = 4 × (V² / R) = 4 × 1 W = 4 W

So the new rate of energy dissipation is 4 W.

[college_physics] Q4  answer=B
A rod measures 1.00 m in its rest system. How fast must an observer move parallel to the rod to measure its length to be 0.80 m?
A. 0.50c
B. 0.60c
C. 0.70c
D. 0.80c
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given:

- Proper length of the rod, \( L_0 = 1.00 \, \text{m} \) (length in its rest frame)
- Observed length, \( L = 0.80 \, \text{m} \)
- We need to find the speed \( v \) of the observer relative to the rod such that length contraction occurs.

The formula for length contraction is:

\[
L = L_0 \sqrt{1 - \frac{v^2}{c^2}}
\]

Plug in the values:

\[
0.80 = 1.00 \cdot \sqrt{1 - \frac{v^2}{c^2}}
\]

\[
0.80 = \sqrt{1 - \frac{v^2}{c^2}}
\]

Square both sides:

\[
0.64 = 1 - \frac{v^2}{c^2}
\]

\[
\frac{v^2}{c^2} = 1 - 0.64 = 0.36
\]

\[
\frac{v}{c} = \sqrt{0.36} = 0.6
\]

So, \( v = 0.60c \)

[college_physics] Q5  answer=B
A heat pump is to extract heat from an outdoor environment at 7°C and heat the environment indoors to 27°C. For each 15,000 J of heat delivered indoors, the smallest amount of work that must be supplied to the heat pump is approximately
A. 500 J
B. 1,000 J
C. 1,100 J
D. 2,000 J
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To find the smallest amount of work required by a heat pump, we use the **Carnot coefficient of performance (COP)** for a heat pump, which is:

\[
\text{COP}_{\text{HP}} = \frac{T_H}{T_H - T_C}
\]

where:
- \( T_H \) is the indoor (hot) temperature in Kelvin,
- \( T_C \) is the outdoor (cold) temperature in Kelvin.

Convert temperatures to Kelvin:
- \( T_H = 27^\circ C = 27 + 273 = 300 \, \text{K} \)
- \( T_C = 7^\circ C = 7 + 273 = 280 \, \text{K} \)

Now calculate COP:
\[
\text{COP} = \frac{300}{300 - 280} = \frac{300}{20} = 15
\]

The COP is also defined as:
\[
\text{COP} = \frac{Q_H}{W}
\]
where \( Q_H = 15,000 \, \text{J} \) is the heat delivered indoors, and \( W \) is the work input.

Solve for \( W \):
\[
W = \frac{Q_H}{\text{COP}} = \frac{15,000}{15} = 1,000 \, \text{J}
\]

Thus, the smallest amount of work required is **1,000 J**.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=352  latency=2.6s

[college_physics] Q6  answer=D
Two students perform an experiment in which they drop a ball from rest from a known height above the ground and measure the speed of the ball just before it strikes the ground. From repeated measurement, the students estimate the uncertainty in the measured speed of the ball to be 10 percent. Which of the following gives the uncertainty in the kinetic energy of the ball? (Assume the uncertainty in the ball’s mass is negligibly small.)
A. 5%
B. 10%
C. 15%
D. 20%
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The kinetic energy of an object is given by the formula:

$$
KE = \frac{1}{2}mv^2
$$

Since the mass $ m $ has negligible uncertainty, the uncertainty in kinetic energy depends only on the uncertainty in velocity $ v $.

For a quantity that depends on $ v^2 $, the relative uncertainty in $ KE $ is approximately **twice** the relative uncertainty in $ v $, because:

$$
\frac{\Delta KE}{KE} \approx 2 \cdot \frac{\Delta v}{v}
$$

Given that the uncertainty in $ v $ is 10%, the uncertainty in $ KE $ is:

$$
2 \times 10\% = 20\%
$$

Answer: D

[college_physics] Q9  answer=D
The energy from electromagnetic waves in equilibrium in a cavity is used to melt ice. If the Kelvin temperature of the cavity is increased by a factor of two, the mass of ice that can be melted in a fixed amount of time is increased by a factor of
A. 2
B. 4
C. 8
D. 16
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The energy radiated by a black body (such as the electromagnetic waves in a cavity) is proportional to the fourth power of its absolute temperature, according to the Stefan-Boltzmann law:

\[
P = \sigma A T^4
\]

where:
- \( P \) is the power radiated,
- \( \sigma \) is the Stefan-Boltzmann constant,
- \( A \) is the surface area,
- \( T \) is the absolute temperature in Kelvin.

If the temperature is increased by a factor of 2, the power increases by a factor of:

\[
(2)^4 = 16
\]

Since the energy available to melt ice is proportional to the power (energy per unit time), the amount of ice melted in a fixed time increases by the same factor.

Therefore, the mass of ice that can be melted increases by a factor of 16.

[college_physics] Q10  answer=D
The quantum efficiency of a photon detector is 0.1. If 100 photons are sent into the detector, one after the other, the detector will detect photons
A. exactly 10 times
B. an average of 10 times, with an rms deviation of about 0.1
C. an average of 10 times, with an rms deviation of about 1
D. an average of 10 times, with an rms deviation of about 3
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The quantum efficiency (QE) of a detector is the probability that a photon will be detected. Here, QE = 0.1, meaning each photon has a 10% chance of being detected.

If 100 photons are sent, the number of detections follows a binomial distribution with parameters n = 100 and p = 0.1.

The expected (average) number of detections is:
E[X] = n × p = 100 × 0.1 = 10

The variance of a binomial distribution is:
Var(X) = n × p × (1 - p) = 100 × 0.1 × 0.9 = 9

The standard deviation (rms deviation) is the square root of the variance:
σ = √9 = 3

So, the detector will detect photons on average 10 times, with an rms deviation of about 3.

Answer: D
→ truth=D  predicted=D  correct=True  tokens=200  latency=1.4s

[college_physics] Q11  answer=A
The emission spectrum of the doubly ionized lithium atom Li++ (Z = 3, A = 7) is identical to that of a hydrogen atom in which all the wavelengths are
A. decreased by a factor of 9
B. decreased by a factor of 49
C. decreased by a factor of 81
D. increased by a factor of 9
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The emission spectrum of a hydrogen-like atom is given by the Rydberg formula:

\[
\frac{1}{\lambda} = R Z^2 \left( \frac{1}{n_1^2} - \frac{1}{n_2^2} \right)
\]

where:
- \( R \) is the Rydberg constant,
- \( Z \) is the atomic number,
- \( n_1 \) and \( n_2 \) are the principal quantum numbers.

For hydrogen (H), \( Z = 1 \), and for doubly ionized lithium (Li²⁺), \( Z = 3 \).

The wavelengths in the Li²⁺ spectrum are related to those in hydrogen by:

\[
\frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{Li}^{2+}}} = R (3)^2 \left( \frac{1}{n_1^2} - \frac{1}{n_2^2} \right) = 9 R \left( \frac{1}{n_1^2} - \frac{1}{n_2^2} \right)
\]

While for hydrogen:

\[
\frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{H}}} = R \left( \frac{1}{n_1^2} - \frac{1}{n_2^2} \right)
\]

So,

\[
\frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{Li}^{2+}}} = 9 \cdot \frac{1}{\lambda_{\text{H}}}
\]

This implies:

\[
\lambda_{\text{Li}^{2+}} = \frac{1}{9} \lambda_{\text{H}}
\]

Thus, the wavelengths in the Li²⁺ spectrum are **decreased by a factor of 9** compared to hydrogen.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=401  latency=2.9s

[college_physics] Q13  answer=C
The suspension cable of a 1,000 kg elevator snaps, sending the elevator moving downward through its shaft. The emergency brakes of the elevator stop the elevator shortly before it reaches the bottom of the shaft. If the elevator fell a distance of 100 m starting from rest, the heat that the brakes must dissipate to bring the elevator safely to rest is
A. 10,000 J
B. 100,000 J
C. 1,000,000 J
D. 10,000,000 J
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To find the heat the brakes must dissipate, we calculate the energy that must be absorbed to stop the elevator. This is equal to the gravitational potential energy lost during the fall (since the elevator starts from rest and ends at rest, all energy is converted to heat).

The formula for gravitational potential energy is:  
PE = mgh  
where:  
- m = 1000 kg  
- g = 9.8 m/s² (approx. 10 m/s² for simplification)  
- h = 100 m  

Using g ≈ 10 m/s²:  
PE = 1000 kg × 10 m/s² × 100 m = 1,000,000 J  

Thus, the brakes must dissipate 1,000,000 J of heat energy.

[college_physics] Q14  answer=B
A proton moves in the +z-direction after being accelerated from rest through a potential difference V. The proton then passes through a region with a uniform electric field E in the +x-direction and a uniform magnetic field B in the +y-direction, but the proton’s trajectory is not affected. If the experiment were repeated using a potential difference of 2V, the proton would then be
A. deflected in the +x-direction
B. deflected in the -x-direction
C. deflected in the +y-direction
D. deflected in the -y-direction
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
When a proton moves through a region with both an electric field and a magnetic field, the net force on it is given by the Lorentz force:

\[
\vec{F} = q(\vec{E} + \vec{v} \times \vec{B})
\]

Given:
- Electric field \( \vec{E} \) is in the +x-direction.
- Magnetic field \( \vec{B} \) is in the +y-direction.
- Proton moves in the +z-direction (velocity \( \vec{v} \) in +z).
- Initially, the trajectory is unaffected → net force is zero.

So the forces must balance:

\[
q\vec{E} + q(\vec{v} \times \vec{B}) = 0
\]

Compute \( \vec{v} \times \vec{B} \):
- \( \vec{v} = v \hat{z} \), \( \vec{B} = B \hat{y} \)
- \( \vec{v} \times \vec{B} = v B (\hat{z} \times \hat{y}) = v B (-\hat{x}) \)
- So \( \vec{v} \times \vec{B} = -vB \hat{x} \)

Thus:
\[
qE \hat{x} + q(-vB \hat{x}) = 0 \Rightarrow qE = qvB \Rightarrow v = \frac{E}{B}
\]

This means the velocity must be exactly \( E/B \) for the forces to cancel.

Now, if the potential difference is doubled to \( 2V \), the proton gains more kinetic energy:

\[
\frac{1}{2}mv^2 = q(2V) = 2qV
\]

Previously, with potential \( V \), \( \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = qV \), so the new velocity \( v' \) satisfies:

\[
\frac{1}{2}mv'^2 = 2 \cdot \frac{1}{2}mv^2 \Rightarrow v'^2 = 2v^2 \Rightarrow v' = \sqrt{2}v
\]

So the new speed is \( \sqrt{2}v \), which is **greater** than the original speed \( v \).

Now compute the magnetic force: \( \vec{F}_B = q(\vec{v} \times \vec{B}) = q v' B (-\hat{x}) \), so magnitude is \( q v' B \)

Electric force: \( \vec{F}_E = qE \hat{x} \)

Since \( v' > v \), and originally \( qE = qvB \), now:

\[
q v' B > q v B = qE
\]

So magnetic force magnitude is greater than electric force, and both are in opposite directions (electric force in +x, magnetic force in -x), so net force is:

\[
F_{\text{net}} = qE - q v' B < 0
\]

Thus net force in the **-x-direction**.

So the proton is deflected in the -x-direction.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=691  latency=5.1s

[college_physics] Q15  answer=B
Blue light of wavelength 480 nanometers is most strongly reflected off a thin film of oil on a glass slide when viewed near normal incidence. Assuming that the index of refraction of the oil is 1.2 and that of the glass is 1.6, what is the minimum thickness of the oil film (other than zero) ?
A. 150 nm
B. 200 nm
C. 300 nm
D. 400 nm
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To determine the minimum thickness of the oil film that causes strong reflection of blue light (wavelength 480 nm) near normal incidence, we use the condition for constructive interference in thin films.

### Given:
- Wavelength in vacuum, λ = 480 nm  
- Index of refraction of oil, n_oil = 1.2  
- Index of refraction of glass, n_glass = 1.6  
- Index of refraction of air, n_air ≈ 1.0  

The oil film is on glass. Light reflects from:
1. Air-oil interface (from lower n to higher n → phase change of 180° or λ/2)
2. Oil-glass interface (oil n = 1.2, glass n = 1.6 → also from lower to higher n → phase change of 180° or λ/2)

So both reflections have a phase change of λ/2. Therefore, the **net phase difference due to reflection is zero**. Constructive interference occurs when the **path difference** is an integer multiple of the wavelength in the oil.

The path difference for light traveling through the oil film and back is 2t, where t is the thickness. But since the light is in the oil, we use the wavelength in oil:

λ_oil = λ / n_oil = 480 nm / 1.2 = 400 nm

For constructive interference (since both reflections have phase change), the condition is:

2t = mλ_oil   (for m = 1, 2, 3, ...)

We want the **minimum thickness** (other than zero), so m = 1:

2t = 400 nm → t = 200 nm

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=379  latency=2.7s

[college_physics] Q16  answer=A
Two simple pendulums A and B consist of identical masses suspended from strings of length L_A and L_B, respectively. The two pendulums oscillate in equal gravitational fields. If the period of pendulum B is twice the period of pendulum A, which of the following is true of the lengths of the two pendulums?
A. L_B = 4L_A
B. L_B = 2L_A
C. L_B = L_A
D. L_B = 1/2 L_A
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The period of a simple pendulum is given by the formula:

T = 2π√(L/g)

where T is the period, L is the length of the pendulum, and g is the acceleration due to gravity.

Given that the period of pendulum B is twice that of pendulum A:

T_B = 2T_A

Substituting into the formula:

2π√(L_B/g) = 2 × 2π√(L_A/g)

Divide both sides by 2π:

√(L_B/g) = 2√(L_A/g)

Square both sides:

L_B/g = 4(L_A/g)

Multiply both sides by g:

L_B = 4L_A

Therefore, the length of pendulum B is four times the length of pendulum A.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=173  latency=1.4s

[college_physics] Q18  answer=D
The driver of a police car hears an echo of the car’s siren from a wall toward which the car is moving with a speed of 3.5 m/s. If the speed of sound is 350 m/s and the frequency of the siren is 600 Hz, the driver hears the echo at a frequency nearest to which of the following?
A. 588 Hz
B. 594 Hz
C. 600 Hz
D. 612 Hz
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
This is a Doppler effect problem involving a moving source (police car) and a stationary reflector (wall), with the driver hearing the echo.

We have:
- Speed of the car, \( v_{\text{car}} = 3.5 \, \text{m/s} \) (toward the wall)
- Speed of sound, \( v = 350 \, \text{m/s} \)
- Source frequency, \( f_0 = 600 \, \text{Hz} \)

The sound is emitted by the car (moving source) toward the wall. The wall reflects the sound, and the car (now moving toward the reflected sound) hears the echo.

This is a two-step Doppler effect:

### Step 1: Frequency observed by the wall
The wall is stationary. The source (car) is moving toward the wall. So the frequency heard by the wall is:

\[
f' = f_0 \left( \frac{v}{v - v_{\text{car}}} \right)
\]

\[
f' = 600 \left( \frac{350}{350 - 3.5} \right) = 600 \left( \frac{350}{346.5} \right)
\]

\[
f' \approx 600 \times 1.010101 \approx 606.06 \, \text{Hz}
\]

### Step 2: Frequency heard by the driver (echo)
Now the wall acts as a source emitting frequency \( f' \), and the car (observer) is moving toward the source (wall). So the observed frequency (echo) is:

\[
f'' = f' \left( \frac{v + v_{\text{car}}}{v} \right)
\]

\[
f'' = 606.06 \left( \frac{350 + 3.5}{350} \right) = 606.06 \left( \frac{353.5}{350} \right)
\]

\[
f'' \approx 606.06 \times 1.01 = 612.12 \, \text{Hz}
\]

So the driver hears the echo at approximately **612 Hz**.

Answer: D
→ truth=D  predicted=D  correct=True  tokens=519  latency=3.9s

[college_physics] Q19  answer=C
Which of the following is true about any system that undergoes a reversible thermodynamic process?
A. There are no changes in the internal energy of the system.
B. The temperature of the system remains constant during the process.
C. The entropy of the system and its environment remains unchanged.
D. The entropy of the system and its environment must increase.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
A reversible thermodynamic process is one that can be reversed without leaving any net change in the system or its surroundings. This implies that the total entropy of the system and its environment remains constant, as no entropy is generated during the process.

Let’s evaluate each option:

A. There are no changes in the internal energy of the system.  
→ False. Internal energy can change (e.g., in a reversible isothermal expansion, internal energy may stay constant, but in other reversible processes like adiabatic, it changes).

B. The temperature of the system remains constant during the process.  
→ False. Temperature can vary (e.g., in reversible adiabatic processes, temperature changes).

C. The entropy of the system and its environment remains unchanged.  
→ True. In a reversible process, the total entropy change (system + surroundings) is zero. No entropy is generated.

D. The entropy of the system and its environment must increase.  
→ False. This describes irreversible processes.

Thus, the correct answer is C.

Answer: C
→ truth=C  predicted=C  correct=True  tokens=214  latency=1.7s

where:

- ε_r is the relative dielectric constant (4.0 in this case).

So:

[conceptual_physics] Q2  answer=B
Consider a ball rolling in a horizontal circular path on the inside surface of a cone. The normal force on the ball
A. is mg
B. is greater than mg always
C. may be greater or less than mg
D. is less than mg always
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The ball is moving in a horizontal circular path on the inside surface of a cone. The forces acting on the ball are gravity (mg, downward) and the normal force from the cone's surface, which acts perpendicular to the surface (at an angle to the vertical). Since the ball is moving in a horizontal circle, there must be a net horizontal force (centripetal force) toward the center of the circle. This horizontal component comes from the horizontal part of the normal force.

The normal force has both vertical and horizontal components. The vertical component of the normal force balances the weight (mg) only if there's no vertical acceleration. However, because the normal force is angled (not vertical), its magnitude must be greater than mg to provide both the upward vertical component (to balance mg) and the horizontal component (to provide centripetal force). Therefore, the normal force is greater than mg.

Answer: B

B. 0.25 g

- Time elapsed = 3:00 pm - noon = 3 hours

After each half-life:

- After 2 hours: 0.500 g → 0.250 g  
- After 3 hours: 0.250 g → 0.125 g  

So, at 3:00 pm, 0.125 g remains.

[conceptual_physics] Q17  answer=A
When an increase in speed doubles the kinetic energy of a moving body its momentum
A. increases but less than doubles
B. doubles
C. more than doubles
D. depends on factors not stated
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Let's analyze the relationship between kinetic energy (KE) and momentum (p).

We know:
- Kinetic energy: $ KE = \frac{1}{2}mv^2 $
- Momentum: $ p = mv $

Suppose the speed doubles. Let's see how KE and p change.

If the speed increases from $ v $ to $ 2v $, then:
- New KE = $ \frac{1}{2}m(2v)^2 = \frac{1}{2}m(4v^2) = 4 \times \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = 4 \times \text{original KE} $

But the question says: **"an increase in speed doubles the kinetic energy"**, not that speed doubles.

So, we want to find what happens to momentum when kinetic energy doubles.

Let’s suppose:
- Original KE: $ KE = \frac{1}{2}mv^2 $
- New KE: $ 2KE = \frac{1}{2}m v_{\text{new}}^2 $

So:
$$
2 \cdot \frac{1}{2}mv^2 = \frac{1}{2}m v_{\text{new}}^2 \Rightarrow v_{\text{new}}^2 = 2v^2 \Rightarrow v_{\text{new}} = \sqrt{2}v
$$

Now, momentum:
- Original momentum: $ p = mv $
- New momentum: $ p_{\text{new}} = m v_{\text{new}} = m \sqrt{2}v = \sqrt{2} \cdot p \approx 1.414p $

So momentum increases by a factor of $ \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414 $, which is **less than double**.

Therefore, when kinetic energy doubles, momentum increases but **less than doubles**.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=407  latency=3.0s

[econometrics] Q1  answer=B
Consider the following AR(2) process:

yt = 1.5 yt-1 - 0.5 yt-2 + ut

This is a
A. Stationary process
B. Unit root process
C. Explosive process
D. Stationary and unit root process
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To determine the nature of the AR(2) process:

yt = 1.5 yt-1 - 0.5 yt-2 + ut

We analyze the characteristic equation:

1 - 1.5z + 0.5z² = 0

Multiply by 2: 2 - 3z + z² = 0 → z² - 3z + 2 = 0

Solve: z = (3 ± √(9 - 8))/2 = (3 ± 1)/2 → z = 2 or z = 1

The roots are z = 1 and z = 2.

A unit root process has a root at z = 1. Since one root is exactly 1, this process has a unit root.

Even though there's another root at z = 2 (which would make it explosive), the presence of a unit root means the process is non-stationary and has a unit root.

Therefore, this is a unit root process.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=215  latency=1.6s

[econometrics] Q3  answer=C
Suppose that we are interested in testing the null hypothesis that a GARCH(2,2) model can be restricted to a process with a constant conditional variance using the likelihood ratio test approach. Which of the following statements are true?
A. The test statistic will follow a chi-squared distribution with 2 degrees of freedom under the null hypothesis
B. The value of the log-likelihood function will almost always be bigger for the restricted model than for the unrestricted model
C. If the relevant values of the log-likelihood functions are -112.3 and -118.4, the value of the test statistic is 12.2
D. The likelihood ratio test compares the slopes of the log-likelihood function at the maximum and at the restricted parameter value.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To evaluate the statements regarding the likelihood ratio test for testing whether a GARCH(2,2) model can be restricted to a constant conditional variance:

- The null hypothesis is that the GARCH parameters are zero (i.e., constant variance), so the restricted model is a constant variance model.
- The unrestricted model is the full GARCH(2,2) model.
- The likelihood ratio test statistic is given by:  
  $ LR = -2 \times (\ln L_{\text{restricted}} - \ln L_{\text{unrestricted}}) $

Let’s analyze each option:

A. The test statistic will follow a chi-squared distribution with 2 degrees of freedom under the null hypothesis.  
- The GARCH(2,2) model has 2 GARCH terms and 2 ARCH terms, so 4 parameters in total. Under the null, these 4 parameters are restricted to zero.  
- The degrees of freedom for the LR test is the number of restrictions, which is 4 (not 2).  
- Therefore, this statement is **false**.

B. The value of the log-likelihood function will almost always be bigger for the restricted model than for the unrestricted model.  
- The unrestricted model is more flexible and should fit the data at least as well as the restricted one.  
- So, $\ln L_{\text{unrestricted}} \geq \ln L_{\text{restricted}}$, meaning the log-likelihood is larger for the unrestricted model.  
- So, this statement is **false**.

C. If the relevant values of the log-likelihood functions are -112.3 and -118.4, the value of the test statistic is 12.2.  
- Let’s compute:  
  $ LR = -2 \times (-118.4 - (-112.3)) = -2 \times (-6.1) = 12.2 $  
- This is correct, assuming the first value is for the unrestricted model (larger log-likelihood).  
- But note: the problem says "the relevant values", but doesn’t specify which is which.  
- However, the unrestricted model should have a higher log-likelihood, so if -112.3 > -118.4, then the unrestricted log-likelihood is -112.3, and restricted is -118.4.  
  $ LR = -2 \times (-118.4 - (-112.3)) = -2 \times (-6.1) = 12.2 $  
- So this calculation is correct. **True**.

D. The likelihood ratio test compares the slopes of the log-likelihood function at the maximum and at the restricted parameter value.  
- This is incorrect. The LR test compares the **values** of the log-likelihood functions, not the slopes.  
- The test statistic is based on the difference in log-likelihoods, not the derivative (slope).  
- So, this is **false**.

Only statement C is correct.

Answer: C

[econometrics] Q4  answer=A
Which of the following statements is INCORRECT concerning the classical hypothesis testing framework?
A. If the null hypothesis is rejected, the alternative is accepted
B. The null hypothesis is the statement being tested while the alternative encompasses the remaining outcomes of interest
C. The test of significance and confidence interval approaches will always give the same conclusions
D. Hypothesis tests are used to make inferences about the population parameters.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Let's analyze each option:

A. If the null hypothesis is rejected, the alternative is accepted  
- This is correct. In classical hypothesis testing, rejecting the null hypothesis leads to accepting the alternative hypothesis.

B. The null hypothesis is the statement being tested while the alternative encompasses the remaining outcomes of interest  
- This is correct. The null hypothesis (H₀) is the default assumption, and the alternative (H₁) represents what we are trying to find evidence for.

C. The test of significance and confidence interval approaches will always give the same conclusions  
- This is incorrect. While they are related and often lead to the same conclusion, they do not always agree. For example, in some complex cases (e.g., when using different types of tests or non-symmetric confidence intervals), the conclusions might differ. Also, confidence intervals provide a range of plausible values, while significance tests give a decision based on a p-value.

D. Hypothesis tests are used to make inferences about the population parameters  
- This is correct. Hypothesis testing is a method to make inferences about population parameters based on sample data.

Therefore, the incorrect statement is C.

Answer: C

[econometrics] Q5  answer=C
Suppose that the value of $R^2$ for an estimated regression model is exactly zero. Which of the following are true?

i) All coefficient estimates on the slopes will be zero

ii) The fitted line will be horizontal with respect to all of the explanatory variables

iii) The regression line has not explained any of the variability of y about its mean value

iv) The intercept coefficient estimate must be zero.
A. (ii) and (iv) only
B. (i) and (iii) only
C. (i), (ii), and (iii) only
D. (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
If $ R^2 = 0 $, it means that the regression model explains none of the variability in the dependent variable $ y $ around its mean. This implies that the model's predictions are no better than simply using the mean of $ y $.

Let’s evaluate each statement:

i) **All coefficient estimates on the slopes will be zero**  
Not necessarily. While $ R^2 = 0 $ means the model explains no variability, the slope coefficients could still be non-zero if the regression line is not perfectly horizontal. However, the **predictions** are not better than the mean. But if the slopes were non-zero, they might still be insignificant, but $ R^2 = 0 $ does not imply all slope coefficients are zero. For example, in a model with two variables, one slope could be non-zero, but the regression still explains no variance due to high error or poor fit. So this is **not necessarily true**.

ii) **The fitted line will be horizontal with respect to all of the explanatory variables**  
This would be true only if all slope coefficients are zero. But again, $ R^2 = 0 $ does not require all slopes to be zero. The model could still have non-zero slopes, but the model fit is poor (e.g., due to noise), so $ R^2 = 0 $. So this is **not necessarily true**.

iii) **The regression line has not explained any of the variability of y about its mean value**  
This is **true** by definition. $ R^2 = 0 $ means that the model explains 0% of the variance in $ y $, so it explains none of the variability around the mean.

iv) **The intercept coefficient estimate must be zero**  
No. The intercept is the predicted value when all explanatory variables are zero. It can be non-zero even if $ R^2 = 0 $. For example, if the model is just the mean of $ y $, the intercept could be the mean, and $ R^2 = 0 $ if the model fits poorly but still has a non-zero intercept. So this is **not necessarily true**.

Only statement **(iii)** is definitely true.

But wait — let’s reconsider.

Actually, if $ R^2 = 0 $, then the **sum of squared residuals (SSR)** equals the **total sum of squares (SST)**, meaning the model's predicted values are equal to the mean of $ y $ for all observations. That is, the fitted values $ \hat{y}_i = \bar{y} $ for all $ i $. This implies that the **fitted line is horizontal** (constant), so all slope coefficients are zero.

Therefore:
- (i) is true: all slope coefficients are zero
- (ii) is true: the fitted line is horizontal (since predictions are constant)
- (iii) is true: no variability explained
- (iv): the intercept is $ \bar{y} $, which is not necessarily zero

So (iv) is **not** necessarily true.

Thus, (i), (ii), and (iii) are true.

Answer: C. (i), (ii), and (iii) only
→ truth=C  predicted=C  correct=True  tokens=669  latency=4.7s

[econometrics] Q6  answer=B
Suppose that 100 separate firms were tested to determine how many of them "beat the market" using a Jensen-type regression, and it is found that 3 fund managers significantly do so. Does this suggest prima facie evidence for stock market inefficiency?
A. Yes
B. No
C. In order to answer this question, you would need to test every fund manager trading in that market
D. There is insufficient information given in the question to draw a conclusion about market efficiency.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The question presents a scenario where 100 firms were tested using a Jensen-type regression, and 3 fund managers were found to significantly "beat the market." The key issue here is whether this result provides prima facie evidence for market inefficiency.

In an efficient market, the probability of a fund manager beating the market by chance alone is low, and the number of such managers should be small. However, with 100 firms tested, even under the null hypothesis of market efficiency (i.e., no true alpha), random chance can lead to a few significant results due to multiple testing. Specifically, at a 5% significance level, we would expect about 5 out of 100 firms to appear significant by chance alone. Since 3 is less than 5, this result is not particularly unusual and could easily occur by random chance.

Moreover, the question does not provide information on the significance level used, the statistical power of the test, or whether the results are adjusted for multiple testing (e.g., Bonferroni correction). Without this, we cannot confidently conclude that the 3 significant results are not due to chance.

Therefore, while the result might be interesting, it does not provide strong prima facie evidence for market inefficiency because it could be explained by random variation.

Answer: B

Which of the following conditions are necessary for a series to be classifiable as a weakly stationary process?

(i) It must have a constant mean

(ii) It must have a constant variance

(iii) It must have constant autocovariances for given lags

(iv) It must have a constant probability distribution
A. (ii) and (iv) only
B. (i) and (iii) only
C. (i), (ii), and (iii) only
D. (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
A weakly stationary (or wide-sense stationary) process requires the following conditions:

(i) Constant mean over time — necessary  
(ii) Constant variance over time — necessary  
(iii) Autocovariances depend only on the lag, not on time — necessary  
(iv) Constant probability distribution — not required for weak stationarity (this is a condition for strict stationarity)

Weak stationarity does not require the entire probability distribution to be constant, only that the first two moments (mean and variance) and autocovariances are time-invariant.

[econometrics] Q8  answer=D
If an estimator is said to have minimum variance, which of the following statements is NOT implied?
A. The probability that the estimate is a long way away from its true value is minimised
B. The estimator is efficient
C. Such an estimator would be termed "best"
D. Such an estimator will always be unbiased
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
An estimator with minimum variance means that among all unbiased estimators, it has the smallest variance. Let's evaluate each option:

A. "The probability that the estimate is a long way away from its true value is minimised" – This is generally true for minimum variance estimators, as lower variance implies the estimates are tightly clustered around the true value.

B. "The estimator is efficient" – An estimator with minimum variance (among unbiased estimators) is considered efficient, so this is implied.

C. "Such an estimator would be termed 'best'" – In the context of unbiased estimators, minimum variance implies it is the "best" in terms of precision, so this is implied.

D. "Such an estimator will always be unbiased" – This is NOT implied. Minimum variance does not guarantee unbiasedness. An estimator can have minimum variance but still be biased (e.g., in biased estimation problems). The minimum variance property is typically discussed in the context of unbiased estimators, but the statement says "will always be unbiased," which is not necessarily true.

Therefore, the statement NOT implied is D.

Answer: D

[econometrics] Q9  answer=D
Which of the following may be consequences of one or more of the CLRM assumptions being violated?

i) The coefficient estimates are not optimal


ii) The standard error estimates are not optimal


iii) The distributions assumed for the test statistics are inappropriate


iv) Conclusions regarding the strength of relationships between the dependent

and independent variables may be invalid.
A. (ii) and (iv) only
B. (i) and (iii) only
C. (i), (ii), and (iii) only
D. (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Violating the Classical Linear Regression Model (CLRM) assumptions can lead to several issues:

i) The coefficient estimates are not optimal: If assumptions like linearity, no perfect multicollinearity, or exogeneity are violated, the OLS estimators may not be Best Linear Unbiased Estimators (BLUE), meaning they are not optimal.

ii) The standard error estimates are not optimal: If assumptions like homoscedasticity or no autocorrelation are violated, the standard errors may be biased, leading to incorrect inference.

iii) The distributions assumed for the test statistics are inappropriate: Violations (e.g., non-normality of errors) can make t-distributions and F-distributions invalid, affecting hypothesis testing.

iv) Conclusions regarding the strength of relationships may be invalid: If the model assumptions are violated, the estimated relationships may be misleading or spurious, making inferences about the strength of relationships unreliable.

All four consequences (i, ii, iii, iv) can result from CLRM assumption violations.

Answer: D

[econometrics] Q10  answer=C
If a Johansen "trace" test for a null hypothesis of 2 cointegrating vectors is applied to a system containing 4 variables is conducted, which eigenvalues would be used in the test?
A. All of them
B. The largest 2
C. The smallest 2
D. The second largest
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The Johansen trace test for cointegration tests the null hypothesis of at most r cointegrating vectors against the alternative of more than r cointegrating vectors. When testing the null hypothesis of 2 cointegrating vectors in a system of 4 variables, the test uses the eigenvalues to determine whether there are more than 2 cointegrating relationships.

In the Johansen procedure, the test statistic is based on the sum of the eigenvalues corresponding to the cointegrating relationships. Specifically, for a null hypothesis of r cointegrating vectors, the test uses the eigenvalues that are associated with the cointegrating vectors. The trace test considers the sum of the eigenvalues from the largest down to the r+1-th eigenvalue.

For a system of 4 variables, there are 4 eigenvalues. When testing the null hypothesis of 2 cointegrating vectors, the test uses the sum of the eigenvalues from the 3rd and 4th (i.e., the smallest two) eigenvalues to compute the test statistic. This is because the test evaluates the significance of additional cointegrating vectors beyond the hypothesized number.

Thus, the eigenvalues used in the test are the smallest 2.

Answer: C
→ truth=C  predicted=C  correct=True  tokens=261  latency=1.9s

[econometrics] Q12  answer=D
Which of the following statements are true concerning maximum likelihood (ML) estimation in the context of GARCH models?

i) Maximum likelihood estimation selects the parameter values that maximise the

probability that we would have actually observed the values of the series y that we

actually did.


ii) GARCH models can only be estimated by ML and not by OLS


iii) For estimation of a standard linear model (with no GARCH), the OLS and ML

estimates for the slope and intercept parameters will be identical but the estimator

for the variance of the disturbances is slightly different


iv) Most computer packages use numerical procedures to estimate GARCH models

rather than a set of analytical formulae
A. (ii) and (iv) only
B. (i) and (iii) only
C. (i), (ii), and (iii) only
D. (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Let's evaluate each statement one by one:

(i) Maximum likelihood estimation selects the parameter values that maximise the probability that we would have actually observed the values of the series y that we actually did.  
- This is correct. ML estimation finds the parameters that make the observed data most probable under the assumed model.

(ii) GARCH models can only be estimated by ML and not by OLS  
- This is true. GARCH models involve estimating parameters in the conditional variance equation, which is nonlinear and cannot be estimated by OLS. ML is required because of the likelihood function based on the distribution of the residuals.

(iii) For estimation of a standard linear model (with no GARCH), the OLS and ML estimates for the slope and intercept parameters will be identical but the estimator for the variance of the disturbances is slightly different  
- This is correct. Under normality, OLS and ML yield the same coefficient estimates, but ML provides a different variance estimate (using MLE of the variance), whereas OLS uses a bias-corrected estimator.

(iv) Most computer packages use numerical procedures to estimate GARCH models rather than a set of analytical formulae  
- This is true. GARCH models typically require numerical optimization (e.g., Newton-Raphson) because the likelihood function does not have a closed-form solution.

All four statements are true.

Answer: D

[econometrics] Q14  answer=D
Consider a series that follows an MA(1) with zero mean and a moving average coefficient of 0.4. What is the value of the autocovariance at lag 1?
A. 0.4
B. 1
C. 0.34
D. It is not possible to determine the value of the autocovariances without knowing the disturbance variance.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
For an MA(1) process with zero mean, the general form is:

$$
X_t = \varepsilon_t + \theta \varepsilon_{t-1}
$$

where $\varepsilon_t$ is white noise with variance $\sigma^2$, and $\theta = 0.4$.

The autocovariance at lag 1 is given by:

$$
\gamma(1) = \text{Cov}(X_t, X_{t-1}) = \text{Cov}(\varepsilon_t + \theta \varepsilon_{t-1}, \varepsilon_{t-1} + \theta \varepsilon_{t-2})
$$

Only the overlapping term $\varepsilon_{t-1}$ appears in both, so:

$$
\gamma(1) = \text{Cov}(\theta \varepsilon_{t-1}, \varepsilon_{t-1}) = \theta \sigma^2
$$

But we don’t know the variance of the disturbance $\sigma^2$, so we cannot compute the numerical value of $\gamma(1)$ without it.

Therefore, the correct answer is:

Answer: D

Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The GARCH(1,1) model is designed to capture volatility clustering and fat tails in financial return time series.

- (iv) Volatility affecting returns: No, this is not directly captured by GARCH models. GARCH models focus on modeling the variance of returns, not how volatility affects the mean return.

[econometrics] Q18  answer=B
Under which of the following situations would bootstrapping be preferred to pure simulation?

i) If it is desired that the distributional properties of the data in the experiment

are the same as those of some actual data


ii) If it is desired that the distributional properties of the data in the experiment

are known exactly


iii) If the distributional properties of the actual data are unknown


iv) If the sample of actual data available is very small
A. (ii) and (iv) only
B. (i) and (iii) only
C. (i), (ii), and (iv) only
D. (i), (ii), (iii), and (iv)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Bootstrapping is a resampling technique used to estimate the distribution of a statistic by sampling with replacement from the observed data. It is particularly useful when the underlying distribution is unknown or when the sample size is small. Let's evaluate each situation:

i) If it is desired that the distributional properties of the data in the experiment are the same as those of some actual data — Bootstrapping preserves the empirical distribution of the observed data, so it is appropriate here.

ii) If it is desired that the distributional properties of the data in the experiment are known exactly — Pure simulation is preferred here because you can specify the exact distribution, unlike bootstrapping which relies on observed data.

iii) If the distributional properties of the actual data are unknown — Bootstrapping is ideal because it doesn't require knowledge of the true distribution.

iv) If the sample of actual data available is very small — Bootstrapping is often used in small samples to estimate variability when parametric assumptions may not hold.

Therefore, bootstrapping is preferred in (i), (iii), and (iv), but not (ii), since pure simulation is better when the distribution is known exactly.

Answer: B. (i) and (iii) only

Which of the following statements is false concerning the linear probability model?
A. There is nothing in the model to ensure that the estimated probabilities lie between zero and one

C. The error terms will be heteroscedastic and not normally distributed
D. The model is much harder to estimate than a standard regression model with a continuous dependent variable
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The linear probability model (LPM) is a regression model used when the dependent variable is binary. Let's evaluate each statement:

A. There is nothing in the model to ensure that the estimated probabilities lie between zero and one  
- True. The LPM does not constrain predicted values to [0,1], so they can fall outside this range.

B. Even if the probabilities are truncated at zero and one, there will probably be many observations for which the probability is either exactly zero or exactly one  
- True. Because the model is linear and can predict values outside [0,1], truncation is often applied, leading to many predicted probabilities at the bounds.

C. The error terms will be heteroscedastic and not normally distributed  
- True. In LPM, the error variance depends on the predicted probability, leading to heteroscedasticity. Errors are not normally distributed because they are Bernoulli (0 or 1), not continuous.

D. The model is much harder to estimate than a standard regression model with a continuous dependent variable  
- False. The LPM is estimated using ordinary least squares (OLS), just like standard regression, so it is not harder to estimate. The computational difficulty is the same.

Therefore, the false statement is D.

For over-identified systems of simultaneous equations, the estimation techniques that are valid and commonly used are:

But note: **ILS is not generally recommended for over-identified systems** because it does not utilize the over-identifying restrictions efficiently. However, technically, **ILS can be applied**, though it's not ideal.

In practice, **2SLS and IV** are the primary methods used for over-identified systems. **ILS is not typically used for over-identified systems**, and **OLS is invalid**.

Which lamp has the best Colour Rendering Index (CRI)?

B. Fluorescent

Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The Colour Rendering Index (CRI) measures how accurately a light source reveals the colors of objects compared to natural light. LEDs and fluorescents typically have lower CRIs (often 80–95 for good quality LEDs, but can be lower), while high-pressure sodium vapour lamps have very poor CRI (around 20–25), making colors appear distorted. The number of output pins in 8085 microprocessors are

Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

[electrical_engineering] Q6  answer=B
A metal surface with 1 meter radius and surface charge density of 20 coulombs / m2 is enclosed in a 10 m side cube. The total outward electric displacement normal to the surface of the cube is
A. 40π coulombs.
B. 80π coulombs.
C. 10π coulombs.
D. none of these.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given:

- A metal surface with radius = 1 meter (so it's a sphere of radius 1 m),
- Surface charge density σ = 20 C/m²,
- This sphere is enclosed inside a cube of side 10 m,
- We are to find the **total outward electric displacement normal to the surface of the cube**.

Let’s recall:

- Electric displacement vector **D** is related to free charge by Gauss’s law:  
  ∮ **D** · d**A** = Q_free enclosed

- The total outward electric displacement through a closed surface is equal to the total **free charge enclosed**.

So, the total outward electric displacement normal to the cube's surface is simply the total charge enclosed by the cube.

Since the metal sphere is enclosed inside the cube, and assuming no other charges, the total charge enclosed is the charge on the sphere.

Charge on the sphere = surface charge density × surface area  
= σ × 4πr²  
= 20 C/m² × 4π(1)²  
= 20 × 4π  
= 80π coulombs

So, the total outward electric displacement through the cube's surface is **80π coulombs**.

Note: The size of the cube (10 m side) does not matter as long as it encloses the sphere — the flux depends only on enclosed charge.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=297  latency=2.2s

[electrical_engineering] Q7  answer=A
If P is the power of a star connected system then what will be power of an equivalent delta connected system?
A. P
B. 3P
C. P/3
D. None of the above
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
In a star-connected system, the power is given by P = √3 × V_line × I_line × cosφ.

In a delta-connected system, the line voltage and line current are related to the phase values differently. For the same line voltage and line current, the power in a delta connection is:

P_delta = √3 × V_line × I_line × cosφ

But the key point is that for the same load impedance, when switching from star to delta, the phase voltage in delta is √3 times the phase voltage in star, and the phase current is also different.

However, the question asks for the power of an *equivalent* delta connected system. "Equivalent" means the same load impedance and same line voltage.

In star connection:  
- Phase voltage = V_line / √3  
- Phase current = I_line  
- Power per phase = (V_line / √3) × I_line × cosφ  
- Total power P = 3 × (V_line / √3) × I_line × cosφ = √3 × V_line × I_line × cosφ

In delta connection:  
- Phase voltage = V_line  
- Phase current = I_phase = V_line / Z  
- But line current I_line = √3 × I_phase  
- So phase power = V_line × (V_line / Z) × cosφ  
- Total power = 3 × V_line × (V_line / Z) × cosφ = 3 × (V_line² / Z) × cosφ

But in star, power = 3 × (V_line² / 3Z) × cosφ = (V_line² / Z) × cosφ

So for same Z and same line voltage, the delta power is 3 times the star power.

Therefore, if P is the power of the star-connected system, the equivalent delta-connected system will have power 3P.

Answer: B
→ truth=A  predicted=B  correct=False  tokens=404  latency=3.0s

Which of the following is a variable displacement transducer?

B. potentiometer
C. synchros
D. none of the above
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
A variable displacement transducer converts mechanical displacement into an electrical signal, where the output varies with the amount of displacement. Let's evaluate each option:

A. Tachometer – Measures rotational speed (RPM), not displacement. It outputs a signal proportional to speed, not position or displacement. So, not a variable displacement transducer.

B. Potentiometer – This device converts linear or rotational displacement into a variable resistance (and thus voltage). It is commonly used as a position sensor, making it a variable displacement transducer.

C. Synchros – These are rotary transformers used to measure angular position, but they are more about angular encoding rather than direct displacement. While they can be used in displacement systems, they are not typically classified as variable displacement transducers in the same sense as potentiometers.

D. None of the above – This would be correct only if all the above were incorrect.

The best answer is B, as a potentiometer directly measures displacement (linear or angular) and outputs a variable signal proportional to that displacement.

[electrical_engineering] Q12  answer=B
A step up chopper has input voltage 110 V and output voltage 150 V. The value of duty cycle is
A. 0.32.
B. 0.67.
C. 0.45.
D. 0.27.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To find the duty cycle of a step-up chopper (also known as a boost converter), we use the formula:

$$
V_{out} = \frac{V_{in}}{1 - D}
$$

Where:
- $ V_{out} $ = output voltage = 150 V
- $ V_{in} $ = input voltage = 110 V
- $ D $ = duty cycle (to be found)

Rearranging the formula:

$$
1 - D = \frac{V_{in}}{V_{out}} = \frac{110}{150} = 0.7333
$$

$$
D = 1 - 0.7333 = 0.2667 \approx 0.27
$$

So, the duty cycle is approximately 0.27.

Answer: D
→ truth=B  predicted=D  correct=False  tokens=190  latency=1.4s

A Lissajous pattern on an oscilloscope has 5 horinzontal tangencies and 2 vertical tangencies. The frequency of horizontal input is 100 Hz. The frequency of the vertical will be
A. 500 Hz.
B. 250 Hz.
C. 400 Hz.
D. 625 Hz.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To determine the frequency of the vertical input in a Lissajous pattern, we use the ratio of the number of horizontal and vertical tangencies.

Given:
- Number of horizontal tangencies = 5
- Number of vertical tangencies = 2
- Frequency of horizontal input (f_x) = 100 Hz

The ratio of frequencies is given by:

f_y / f_x = (number of horizontal tangencies) / (number of vertical tangencies)

So,

f_y / 100 = 5 / 2

f_y = 100 × (5 / 2) = 250 Hz

Therefore, the vertical frequency is 250 Hz.

Stability of a transmission line can be increased by
A. shunt capacitor
B. series capacitor
C. shunt reactor
D. both A and B
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The stability of a transmission line can be improved by compensating for reactive power. 

- A shunt capacitor injects reactive power into the system, improving voltage regulation and enhancing stability.
- A series capacitor reduces the line's inductive reactance, increasing power transfer capability and improving transient stability.
- A shunt reactor absorbs reactive power, which can be useful in certain scenarios but generally reduces stability compared to capacitive compensation.

Thus, both shunt and series capacitors improve stability.

[electrical_engineering] Q16  answer=C
The solid angle subtended by an area of 2400 cm^2 on the surface of a sphere of diameter 1.2 m is
A. 3 / 2.
B. 1 / 3.
C. 2 / 3.
D. 2 / 5.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
To find the solid angle subtended by an area on the surface of a sphere, we use the formula:

\[
\Omega = \frac{A}{r^2}
\]

where:
- \( \Omega \) is the solid angle in steradians,
- \( A \) is the area on the sphere's surface,
- \( r \) is the radius of the sphere.

Given:
- Area \( A = 2400 \, \text{cm}^2 = 2400 \times 10^{-4} \, \text{m}^2 = 0.24 \, \text{m}^2 \),
- Diameter = 1.2 m → radius \( r = 0.6 \, \text{m} \).

Now compute:

\[
\Omega = \frac{0.24}{(0.6)^2} = \frac{0.24}{0.36} = \frac{2}{3}
\]

So the solid angle is \( \frac{2}{3} \) steradians.

[electrical_engineering] Q18  answer=C
The feedback factor of a Wien bridge oscillator using Op-Amp is
A. 1/3
B. 1/4
C. 1/2
D. 1
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The feedback factor (β) of a Wien bridge oscillator using an Op-Amp is determined by the voltage divider network in the feedback circuit. The Wien bridge oscillator uses a series RC and parallel RC network in the positive feedback path. The feedback factor β is the ratio of the voltage at the non-inverting input to the output voltage.

For the Wien bridge oscillator, the feedback factor β is given by:

β = V⁺ / V_out = 1 / (3 + j(ωRC - 1/(ωRC)))

At the oscillation frequency (where ωRC = 1), the imaginary part becomes zero, and the feedback factor simplifies to:

β = 1 / 3

Thus, the feedback factor is 1/3.

Answer: A

[formal_logic] Q1  answer=C
 Select the best translation into predicate logic: Ms. Jackson lent me some sugar, but neither Janet nor Latoya did.
A. Lm ∨ ~(Jl ∨ Ll)
B. Lm • ~(Lj • Ll)
C. Lm • ~(Lj ∨ Ll)
D. Lm ∨ ~(Lj • Ll)
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Let's analyze the sentence: "Ms. Jackson lent me some sugar, but neither Janet nor Latoya did."

This means:
- Ms. Jackson lent me sugar: Lm (where Lm = "Ms. Jackson lent me sugar")
- Neither Janet nor Latoya did: so both Janet and Latoya did not lend me sugar. That is: ¬(Jl ∨ Ll), which is equivalent to ¬Jl ∧ ¬Ll.

So the full statement is: Lm and (neither Janet nor Latoya lent me sugar) → Lm ∧ ¬(Jl ∨ Ll)

In predicate logic, this is: Lm • ~(Jl ∨ Ll)

Now look at the options:
A. Lm ∨ ~(Jl ∨ Ll) — this is "Lm OR not (Jl or Ll)" — wrong connective
B. Lm • ~(Lj • Ll) — this is "Lm and not (Lj and Ll)" — that would mean Janet and Latoya didn't both lend, but one could have — not "neither"
C. Lm • ~(Jl ∨ Ll) — this is "Lm and not (Jl or Ll)" — means both didn't lend — correct
D. Lm ∨ ~(Lj • Ll) — disjunction, wrong

So the correct answer is C.

Answer: C
→ truth=C  predicted=C  correct=True  tokens=301  latency=2.4s

[formal_logic] Q3  answer=A
 Use the following key to translate the given formula of PL to natural, English sentences.
A: Marina reads a Percy Jackson book.
B: Izzy plays Minecraft.
C: Emily stops working.
D: Russell makes dinner.
E: Ashleigh stops by.
~(A ⊃ B) • (B ⊃ ~E)
A. It's not the case that Marina's reading a Percy Jackson book entails that Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy's playing Minecraft does entail that Ashleigh doesn't stop by.
B. If Marina doesn't read a Percy Jackson book, then Izzy plays Minecraft, which entails that Ashleigh doesn't stop by.
C. Marina's reading a Percy Jackson book does not entail that Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy plays Minecraft provided that Ashleigh doesn't stop by.
D. It's not true that Marina reads a Percy Jackson book only when Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy plays Minecraft only when Ashleigh stops by.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Let's break down the logical formula:  
~(A ⊃ B) • (B ⊃ ~E)

First, recall that:  
- A ⊃ B means "If A, then B" (A implies B)  
- ~(A ⊃ B) means "It is not the case that if A then B"  
  This is logically equivalent to A • ~B (A is true and B is false)  
- B ⊃ ~E means "If B, then not E"  
- The whole formula is: ~(A ⊃ B) and (B ⊃ ~E)

So:  
- ~(A ⊃ B) → Marina reads a Percy Jackson book (A is true), but Izzy does **not** play Minecraft (B is false)  
- (B ⊃ ~E) → If Izzy plays Minecraft, then Ashleigh does **not** stop by  

Now, look at the options:

A. "It's not the case that Marina's reading a Percy Jackson book entails that Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy's playing Minecraft does entail that Ashleigh doesn't stop by."  
→ This matches:  
- ~(A ⊃ B): It's not true that A implies B → correct  
- (B ⊃ ~E): B implies not E → correct  
This is accurate.

B. "If Marina doesn't read a Percy Jackson book, then Izzy plays Minecraft, which entails that Ashleigh doesn't stop by."  
→ This is ~A ⊃ B • (B ⊃ ~E), which is not the same. Incorrect.

C. "Marina's reading a Percy Jackson book does not entail that Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy plays Minecraft provided that Ashleigh doesn't stop by."  
→ The first part is correct (~(A ⊃ B)), but the second part says (B ⊃ ~E) is equivalent to (B if ~E), which is not the same as "B provided that ~E" (which would be ~E ⊃ B). Wrong direction.

D. "It's not true that Marina reads a Percy Jackson book only when Izzy plays Minecraft, but Izzy plays Minecraft only when Ashleigh stops by."  
→ "Only when" means B ⊃ A for the first, but that's not what's written. Also, "Izzy plays Minecraft only when Ashleigh stops by" means B ⊃ E, but we have B ⊃ ~E. So incorrect.

Thus, only A correctly captures the logic.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=528  latency=3.8s

[formal_logic] Q4  answer=B
 Construct a complete truth table for the following argument. Then, using the truth table, determine whether the argument is valid or invalid. If the argument is invalid, choose an option which presents a counterexample. (There may be other counterexamples as well.)
T ∨ U
W · T / U
A. Valid
B. Invalid. Counterexample when T and W are true and U is false
C. Invalid. Counterexample when T is true and W and U are false
D. Invalid. Counterexample when W is true and T and U are false
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given the argument:

Premise 1: T ∨ U  
Premise 2: W · T  
Conclusion: U  

We are to construct a truth table and determine validity.

First, list all atomic propositions: T, U, W. So 2³ = 8 rows.

We need to evaluate:
- Premise 1: T ∨ U
- Premise 2: W · T (which is W ∧ T)
- Conclusion: U

The argument is **valid** if and only if in **every row where both premises are true**, the conclusion is also true.

Let's construct the truth table:

| T | U | W | T ∨ U | W ∧ T | U (conclusion) |
|---|---|---|-------|-------|----------------|
| T | T | T |   T   |   T   |       T        |
| T | T | F |   T   |   F   |       T        |
| T | F | T |   T   |   T   |       F        |
| T | F | F |   T   |   F   |       F        |
| F | T | T |   T   |   F   |       T        |
| F | T | F |   T   |   F   |       T        |
| F | F | T |   F   |   F   |       F        |
| F | F | F |   F   |   F   |       F        |

Now, find rows where **both premises are true**:

- Premise 1: T ∨ U is true
- Premise 2: W ∧ T is true

Look for rows where both are true:

- Row 1: T= T, U= T, W= T → T ∨ U = T, W ∧ T = T → both true → conclusion U = T → OK
- Row 3: T= T, U= F, W= T → T ∨ U = T, W ∧ T = T → both true → conclusion U = F → **invalid!**

So, there is a **counterexample** where:
- T = true
- W = true
- U = false

In this case, both premises are true, but conclusion U is false.

So the argument is **invalid**, and the counterexample is: T true, W true, U false.

Now check options:

A. Valid → Incorrect  
B. Invalid. Counterexample when T and W are true and U is false → **Matches**  
C. Invalid. Counterexample when T is true and W and U are false → W false, so W ∧ T = F → premise 2 false → not a counterexample  
D. Invalid. Counterexample when W is true and T and U are false → T false → W ∧ T = F → premise 2 false → not a counterexample

Only B correctly identifies a valid counterexample.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=626  latency=4.4s

[formal_logic] Q5  answer=B
 Which of the following propositions is an immediate (one-step) consequence in PL of the given premises?
A ⊃ (B • C)
~(B • C)
A ∨ (D ∨ B)
A. A
B. ~A
C. B
D. ~B
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given the following premises in propositional logic (PL):

1. A ⊃ (B • C)  
2. ~(B • C)  
3. A ∨ (D ∨ B)

We are to determine which of the following is an **immediate (one-step) consequence** of these premises:

A. A  
B. ~A  
C. B  
D. ~B

Let’s analyze step by step.

From premise 1: A ⊃ (B • C)  
From premise 2: ~(B • C)

By **modus tollens**, from A ⊃ (B • C) and ~(B • C), we can infer **~A**.

Modus tollens:  
If A ⊃ (B • C)  
and ~(B • C)  
then ~A

This is a one-step inference.

So, **~A** is an immediate consequence.

Now, check the other options:

A. A – This would contradict ~A, so it's not a consequence.  
C. B – We don’t have any direct information about B alone. We only know ~(B • C), which means at least one of B or C is false, but we don’t know which. So we cannot conclude B.  
D. ~B – ~(B • C) means that it's not the case that both B and C are true. So either ~B or ~C (or both). But we can't conclude ~B specifically from the premises.

Therefore, only **~A** is a valid one-step consequence.

Answer: B
→ truth=B  predicted=B  correct=True  tokens=330  latency=2.4s

[formal_logic] Q7  answer=C
 Construct a complete truth table for the following pairs of propositions. Then, using the truth tables, determine whether the statements are logically equivalent or contradictory. If neither, determine whether they are consistent or inconsistent. Justify your answers.
(G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G and ~G ≡ (~H · G)
A. Logically equivalent
B. Contradictory
C. Neither logically equivalent nor contradictory, but consistent
D. Inconsistent
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given two propositions:

1. (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G  
2. ~G ≡ (~H · G)

We need to construct a truth table for both and determine their logical relationship: logically equivalent, contradictory, consistent, or inconsistent.

### Step 1: Identify variables
The propositions involve two propositional variables: G and H.

So, there are 2² = 4 possible truth value combinations:

| G | H |
|---|---|
| T | T |
| T | F |
| F | T |
| F | F |

We will evaluate both expressions for each row.

---

### Step 2: Evaluate (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G

We break it down:

- ~H is the negation of H
- G ∨ ~H is the disjunction
- Then (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G is a material implication

Recall: P ⊃ Q is false only when P is true and Q is false.

Let’s compute:

| G | H | ~H | G ∨ ~H | (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G |
|---|---|----|--------|-------------|
| T | T | F  | T ∨ F = T | T ⊃ T = T |
| T | F | T  | T ∨ T = T | T ⊃ T = T |
| F | T | F  | F ∨ F = F | F ⊃ F = T |
| F | F | T  | F ∨ T = T | T ⊃ F = F |

So the truth values for (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G are: T, T, T, F

---

### Step 3: Evaluate ~G ≡ (~H · G)

This is a biconditional: ~G ≡ (~H ∧ G)

Recall: P ≡ Q is true when both P and Q have the same truth value.

Compute:

- ~G: negation of G
- ~H: negation of H
- ~H · G: (~H ∧ G)
- Then compare ~G and (~H ∧ G)

| G | H | ~G | ~H | ~H · G | ~G ≡ (~H · G) |
|---|---|----|----|--------|--------------|
| T | T | F  | F  | F · T = F | F ≡ F = T |
| T | F | F  | T  | T · T = T | F ≡ T = F |
| F | T | T  | F  | F · F = F | T ≡ F = F |
| F | F | T  | T  | T · F = F | T ≡ F = F |

So the truth values for ~G ≡ (~H · G) are: T, F, F, F

---

### Step 4: Compare the two truth tables

| Row | (G ∨ ~H) ⊃ G | ~G ≡ (~H · G) |
|-----|-------------|--------------|
| 1   | T           | T            |
| 2   | T           | F            |
| 3   | T           | F            |
| 4   | F           | F            |

Now analyze:

- Are they **logically equivalent**? No — they do not have identical truth values in all rows. For example, row 2: one is T, one is F.
- Are they **contradictory**? No — contradictory means they have opposite truth values in every row. But row 1: both T, so not contradictory.
- Are they **consistent**? Yes — they are consistent if there is **at least one row** where both are true. Row 1: both T → consistent.
- Are they **inconsistent**? No — because they are not always false together, and not contradictory.

So, they are **not logically equivalent**, **not contradictory**, but **consistent**.

Answer: C
→ truth=C  predicted=C  correct=True  tokens=856  latency=6.0s

============================================================
[formal_logic] Q8  answer=A
Construct a complete truth table for the following argument. Then, using the truth table, determine whether the argument is valid or invalid. If the argument is invalid, choose an option which presents a counterexample. (There may be other counterexamples as well.)
~(M ≡ ~N)
M / N
A. Valid
B. Invalid. Counterexample when M and N are true
C. Invalid. Counterexample when M is true and N is false
D. Invalid. Counterexample when N is true and M is false
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given the argument:

Premise 1: ~(M ≡ ~N)  
Premise 2: M  
Conclusion: N

We need to construct a truth table for all possible truth values of M and N, evaluate the premises and conclusion, and determine whether the argument is valid.

### Step 1: Understand the components

- M ≡ ~N means M is logically equivalent to not N. That is, M and ~N have the same truth value.
- ~(M ≡ ~N) means that M is **not** equivalent to ~N — so they have different truth values.
- Premise 2: M is true.
- Conclusion: N

We want to check if whenever both premises are true, the conclusion is also true. If there is a case where both premises are true but N is false, the argument is invalid.

### Step 2: Construct truth table

We consider all combinations of M and N:

| M | N | ~N | M ≡ ~N | ~(M ≡ ~N) | M | N |
|---|---|----|--------|----------|---|---|
| T | T | F  |  F     | T        | T | T |
| T | F | T  |  T     | F        | T | F |
| F | T | F  |  F     | T        | F | T |
| F | F | T  |  T     | F        | F | F |

Now, evaluate the argument:

- Premise 1: ~(M ≡ ~N) — we need this to be true.
- Premise 2: M — we need this to be true.
- Conclusion: N — we check if it must be true when both premises are true.

Now go row by row:

1. M = T, N = T:
   - ~N = F
   - M ≡ ~N → T ≡ F → F
   - ~(M ≡ ~N) → T ✅
   - M = T ✅
   - N = T ✅
   → All true, conclusion holds.

2. M = T, N = F:
   - ~N = T
   - M ≡ ~N → T ≡ T → T
   - ~(M ≡ ~N) → F ❌
   - So premise 1 is false → this row does not satisfy both premises.

3. M = F, N = T:
   - ~N = F
   - M ≡ ~N → F ≡ F → T
   - ~(M ≡ ~N) → F ❌
   - M = F ❌
   → Both premises false → irrelevant.

4. M = F, N = F:
   - ~N = T
   - M ≡ ~N → F ≡ T → F
   - ~(M ≡ ~N) → T ✅
   - M = F ❌
   → Premise 2 false → irrelevant.

So the only row where **both premises are true** is the first one: M = T, N = T.

In that row, N is true, so conclusion holds.

Wait — is there any row where both premises are true and N is false?

Let’s double-check row 2:
- M = T, N = F
- ~N = T
- M ≡ ~N → T ≡ T → T
- ~(M ≡ ~N) → F
So premise 1 is false → not a case where both premises are true.

So the only case where both premises are true is when M = T, N = T.

So the argument is **valid** — there is no counterexample.

But let’s double-check the logic.

We have:
- ~(M ≡ ~N) means M and ~N have different truth values.

So:
- If M is true, then ~N must be false → N is true
- If M is false, then ~N must be true → N is false

So ~(M ≡ ~N) is equivalent to M ≡ N

Wait — let’s verify:

M ≡ ~N is false when M and ~N differ.

So:
- M ≡ ~N is false when M is T and ~N is F → N is T → M=T, N=T
- M ≡ ~N is false when M is F and ~N is T → N is F → M=F, N=F

So ~(M ≡ ~N) is true when:
- M=T, N=T → M ≡ ~N is T ≡ F = F → so ~(F) = T ✅
- M=F, N=F → F ≡ T = F → ~(F) = T ✅

So ~(M ≡ ~N) is true when M and N are **both true** or **both false**

So ~(M ≡ ~N) ≡ (M ≡ N)

Yes — because M ≡ ~N is false exactly when M and N are the same.

So ~(M ≡ ~N) is true iff M ≡ N

Therefore, premise 1: ~(M ≡ ~N) is equivalent to M ≡ N

So the argument is:
- M ≡ N
- M
- Therefore N

This is valid — modus ponens.

So the argument is valid.

But let’s check the answer choices.

A. Valid  
B. Invalid. Counterexample when M and N are true  
C. Invalid. Counterexample when M is true and N is false  
D. Invalid. Counterexample when N is true and M is false

But if M and N are both true, both premises are true and conclusion is true — not a counterexample.

If M is true and N is false, then ~(M ≡ ~N) = ~(T ≡ T) = ~(T) = F — so premise 1 false — not a case where both premises are true.

So there is **no counterexample** — the argument is valid.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=1279  latency=9.2s

[formal_logic] Q9  answer=A
Use indirect truth tables to determine whether the following argument is valid. If the argument is invalid, choose an option which presents a counterexample. (There may be other counterexamples as well.)
E ⊃ F
G ⊃ ~F / ~G ∨ ~E
A. Valid
B. Invalid. Counterexample when E, F, and G are true
C. Invalid. Counterexample when E and G are true and F is false
D. Invalid. Counterexample when F and G are true and E is false
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given the argument:

1. E ⊃ F  
2. G ⊃ ~F  
Therefore: ~G ∨ ~E

We want to determine whether this argument is valid using **indirect truth tables**.  
We assume the premises are **true** and the conclusion is **false**, and see if this leads to a contradiction. If it does, the argument is valid. If not, it's invalid, and we can identify a counterexample.

---

### Step 1: Assume the conclusion is false
Conclusion: ~G ∨ ~E is false  
For a disjunction to be false, **both disjuncts must be false**.  
So:  
- ~G is false → G is **true**  
- ~E is false → E is **true**

So:  
- E = true  
- G = true  

---

### Step 2: Check if the premises can be true under this assignment

Premise 1: E ⊃ F  
E = true, so for E ⊃ F to be true, F must be **true**.  
So F = true

Premise 2: G ⊃ ~F  
G = true, so for this to be true, ~F must be true → F = false

But we have a contradiction:  
From Premise 1: F must be true  
From Premise 2: F must be false

So it's **impossible** for both premises to be true when the conclusion is false.

Therefore, the assumption that the conclusion is false leads to a contradiction.  
Thus, the argument is **valid**.

Wait — but let’s double-check. The question says: "If the argument is invalid, choose an option which presents a counterexample."

But our analysis shows the argument is **valid**, so answer should be A.

But let’s check the options again:

A. Valid  
B. Invalid. Counterexample when E, F, and G are true  
C. Invalid. Counterexample when E and G are true and F is false  
D. Invalid. Counterexample when F and G are true and E is false

We just showed that assuming the conclusion is false forces E = true, G = true, then F must be true (from premise 1), but F must be false (from premise 2), so contradiction. So the argument is **valid**.

But let's test option B: E, F, G all true.

Premise 1: E ⊃ F → true ⊃ true = true  
Premise 2: G ⊃ ~F → true ⊃ false = false → premise 2 false  
So premises not both true → not a counterexample.

Option C: E = true, G = true, F = false  
Premise 1: E ⊃ F → true ⊃ false = false → invalid  
So premises not both true.

Option D: F = true, G = true, E = false  
Premise 1: E ⊃ F → false ⊃ true = true  
Premise 2: G ⊃ ~F → true ⊃ false = false → again, premise 2 false.

So no assignment makes both premises true and conclusion false.

Thus, the argument is valid.

Answer: A
→ truth=A  predicted=A  correct=True  tokens=678  latency=4.9s

[formal_logic] Q10  answer=D
 Construct a complete truth table for the following argument. Then, using the truth table, determine whether the argument is valid or invalid. If the argument is invalid, choose an option which presents a counterexample. (There may be other counterexamples as well.)
(G ≡ H) · ~I
~G ∨ (~H ∨ I) / G
A. Valid
B. Invalid. Counterexample when H and I are true and G is false
C. Invalid. Counterexample when H is true and I and G are false
D. Invalid. Counterexample when H, I, and G are false
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
We are given the argument:

Premise 1: (G ≡ H) · ~I

Conclusion: G

We need to construct a truth table for all possible truth values of G, H, and I, evaluate the premises and conclusion, and determine validity.

There are 3 variables → 8 rows.

Let’s construct the truth table.

| G | H | I | G ≡ H | ~I | (G ≡ H) · ~I | ~G | ~H | ~H ∨ I | ~G ∨ (~H ∨ I) | Conclusion G |
|---|---|---|-------|----|-------------|----|----|--------|--------------|-------------|

| T | T | F |   T   | T  |      T      | F  | F  |   F    |      F       |      F      |

| T | F | F |   F   | T  |      F      | F  | T  |   T    |      T       |      T      |
| F | T | T |   F   | F  |      F      | T  | F  |   T    |      T       |      F      |
| F | T | F |   F   | T  |      F      | T  | F  |   F    |      F       |      F      |
| F | F | T |   T   | F  |      F      | T  | T  |   T    |      T       |      T      |
| F | F | F |   T   | T  |      T      | T  | T  |   T    |      T       |      T      |

- (G ≡ H) · ~I = T

- I = F
