[professional_law] Q13  answer=B
A man was prosecuted for the alleged murder of his estranged wife. At trial, the government was allowed to present the testimony of several women who testified to having flirtatious or sexual encounters with the defendant over the past 20 years prior to the homicide. None of the testimony showed any such activity within three years of the murder date, and none of it showed any violent tendencies of the defendant or intentions expressed that he wanted to kill his wife. The defendant had an alibi for the time of the crime and presented many alibi witnesses. The government claimed that it was showing motive by proving the defendant's proclivity for extra-marital affairs over the years and therefore his bad relationship with his wife. The jury convicted him after hearing the testimony of 10 women regarding defendant's various sexual contacts with them over prior years. The defendant appealed the murder conviction, claiming that the women's testimony was prejudicial and inflammatory and showed only bad character, which is not admissible under evidentiary rules. Will the appeals court likely reverse the conviction and order a new trial?
A. Yes, the trial was unfair in presenting testimony of women going back over 20 years; it would have been proper to admit such testimony only as to the five years preceding the crime.
B. Yes, the defendant did not receive a fair trial; the testimony of the women only showed his bad character but because they were old incidents and not continuing, they did not allow a permissible inference that these affairs gave him a motive to kill his spouse.
C. No, if the testimony was admitted in error it was harmless error that did not sway the jury one way or another and so the defendant got a fair trial in all other respects.
D. No, the testimony was admissible because it tended to show a pattern of "transferred intent" that proved the defendant's great hostility to his wife, and his need to eliminate her from his life so that he could go back to having a wild life.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The key issue here is whether the testimony of the women regarding the defendant's past extramarital affairs was admissible. Under evidentiary rules, evidence of a defendant's bad character or prior bad acts is generally inadmissible to show propensity to commit a crime. However, such evidence may be admissible if it is relevant to show motive, opportunity, intent, preparation, plan, knowledge, or identity.

In this case, the government argued that the testimony showed motive—specifically, that the defendant's long history of affairs demonstrated a bad relationship with his wife and thus a motive to kill her. However, the testimony was from incidents over 20 years prior, none within three years of the murder, and none involving violence or threats. The evidence is therefore weak in showing a current motive or intent.

The Supreme Court has held in cases like *Old Chief v. United States* and *Rule 404(b)* of the Federal Rules of Evidence that prior bad acts are only admissible if they are relevant to a material issue and not merely to show character. Here, the evidence appears to be more prejudicial than probative, especially since the defendant had a strong alibi and no evidence of recent hostility or intent.

Moreover, the testimony of multiple women from decades ago, without a clear connection to the murder, risks inflaming the jury and undermining fairness. Given that the evidence was likely irrelevant to motive in the context of the crime and possibly inflammatory, the appeals court would likely find that the error was not harmless and could have influenced the jury.

[professional_law] Q14  answer=A
An on-duty Police Officer hears breaking glass and a blaring alarm, and sees Stan run out of a darkened liquor store at 3 a.m. Stan runs around the corner, out of Officer's view, and hops into a car. Officer sees Stan drive away and pursues, lights flashing. Stan pulls over and Officer arrests him. Officer sees nothing in the passenger compartment, but pops the trunk and finds cash and a baseball bat. Stan's motion to suppress the cash and baseball bat as evidence will likely be
A. Denied, because the Officer had probable cause to search the trunk.
B. Denied, because a search incident to a valid arrest in an automobile always includes the trunk of the automobile.
C. Granted, because the trunk area was out of the range of Stan's control.
D. Granted, because the arrest was illegal and therefore the evidence is considered to be fruit of the poisonous tree.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The officer observed Stan fleeing a liquor store at 3 a.m. with breaking glass and an alarm, which provides probable cause for a criminal act. The officer then pursued and arrested Stan. After the arrest, the officer searched the trunk of the vehicle and found cash and a baseball bat. 

The key issue is whether the search of the trunk was lawful. Under U.S. Supreme Court precedent, such as Chimel v. California and Arizona v. Gant, a search incident to a lawful arrest may include the area within the immediate control of the arrestee. In automobiles, this typically includes the passenger compartment but not necessarily the trunk unless the arrestee is within reach of it or the vehicle is searched incident to arrest. However, in Gant, the Court held that a search of the passenger compartment is permitted if it is incident to arrest and the arrestee is unsecured and within reaching distance of the vehicle's interior. 

The trunk, however, is generally not within the arrestee's immediate control unless there is a specific reason to believe it contains evidence or weapons. Here, the officer did not see anything in the passenger compartment and only searched the trunk after arrest. Since there was no indication the trunk was accessible or related to the arrest, and the officer lacked a warrant or exigent circumstances, the search may be unlawful.

However, the arrest itself was likely valid based on probable cause from the circumstances. But the trunk search may still be unreasonable unless it fits within an exception. The most relevant exception here is not clearly met, as the arrest was in a vehicle, but the trunk is not part of the "immediate area" unless the arrestee could have reached it. The Court in Gant allowed searches of the passenger compartment, but not the trunk, unless the arrestee could have accessed it. Here, the officer found the trunk after the arrest, and there is no indication of exigency or evidence in the trunk being within reach.

Thus, the motion to suppress the evidence (cash and bat) is likely granted because the trunk search was not justified under the circumstances. The evidence may be "fruit of the poisonous tree" if the arrest were illegal, but the arrest was likely legal. However, the search of the trunk may be an illegal search independent of the arrest.

But the better argument is that the search of the trunk was not incident to a lawful arrest, since the arrestee was no longer in the car and the trunk is not within "immediate control." Therefore, the search was likely unlawful, and the motion to suppress would be granted.

Answer: C

A. improper, because it is not relevant to the issues in the case.

[professional_law] Q17  answer=C
An investor from State A filed an action against his State B stockbroker in federal court in State A. The summons and complaint were served at the stockbroker's office in State B, where the process server handed the documents to the stockbroker's administrative assistant. The stockbroker has answered the complaint, asserting the defense of improper service of process. Assume that both states' requirements for service of process are identical to the requirements of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. Is the court likely to dismiss the action for improper service of process?
A. No, because service was made on a person of suitable age found at the stockbroker's place of employment.
B. No, because the stockbroker waived her claim for improper service of process by asserting it in her answer.
C. Yes, because an individual defendant may not be served by delivering process to a third party found at the defendant's place of employment.
D. Yes, because the process of State A courts is not effective in State B.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The key issue is whether service of process was proper under the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure (FRCP). Under FRCP 4(e)(1), service on an individual may be made by delivering a copy of the summons and complaint to the individual personally, or by leaving it with a person of suitable age and discretion at the individual's dwelling or usual place of abode. Alternatively, FRCP 4(h)(1)(A) allows service on an individual by delivering the summons and complaint to an agent authorized to receive service on the individual's behalf, or to a person at the individual's residence or place of business.

Here, service was made at the stockbroker's office in State B, and the process server handed the documents to the stockbroker's administrative assistant. This is not the same as serving the stockbroker personally or through an authorized agent. However, under FRCP 4(e)(1), service can be made by leaving the documents with a "person of suitable age and discretion" at the individual's place of business, which includes a workplace.

The administrative assistant, being at the workplace, qualifies as a "person of suitable age and discretion" at the stockbroker's place of business. Therefore, service was proper under FRCP 4(e)(1). Additionally, the stockbroker answered the complaint and raised the defense of improper service, but under FRCP 12(h)(1), failure to raise the defense of improper service in a timely manner (such as in a motion to dismiss) can result in waiver. However, the stockbroker did raise it in her answer, so it is not waived.

But the question is whether the court is likely to dismiss for improper service. Given that the administrative assistant is at the stockbroker’s place of employment, and service was made to a person of suitable age and discretion at the place of business, service is proper under FRCP 4(e)(1). Therefore, the court is not likely to dismiss for improper service.

[professional_law] Q20  answer=A
A passenger on a subway car placed her pocketbook on the seat next to her and began to zip up her jacket. Defendant was standing in front of the passenger and holding on to the hand rail. Seeing the pocketbook unattended, he suddenly grabbed it and tried to run away. As he did so, the passenger became very frightened and fell backward. She struck her head against the window and was knocked unconscious. In the commotion, Defendant dropped the pocketbook and hurriedly exited the subway car empty-handed. Defendant should be found guilty of
A. larceny.
B. attempted larceny.
C. assault.
D. robbery.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The defendant grabbed the pocketbook while the passenger was present and unattended, which constitutes a taking of property. The defendant's intent to permanently deprive the owner of the property is evident. However, he did not complete the theft because he dropped the pocketbook and fled empty-handed. The passenger's fear and injury were caused by the defendant's actions, but the primary offense was the attempt to steal.

The key legal distinction here is between larceny and attempted larceny. Larceny requires the completed taking and carrying away of property. Since the defendant did not successfully carry away the pocketbook, he did not complete larceny. However, he took a substantial step toward committing larceny by grabbing the pocketbook and attempting to flee, which satisfies the elements of attempted larceny.

The defendant did not use force or threat against the person to obtain the property, so robbery (which requires force or threat) does not apply. Assault requires intent to cause harm or apprehension of imminent harm, but the primary act here was theft, not assault. Therefore, the most appropriate charge is attempted larceny.

According to Macnamara's (2005) pyramid of evaluation, at which stage are opinion polls most useful?

Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
Macnamara's (2005) pyramid of evaluation outlines four stages: Inputs, Outputs, Outtakes, and Outcomes. Opinion polls are most useful at the **Outputs** stage, where they help measure the immediate results or products of a program, such as public awareness or satisfaction. They are not typically used to assess inputs (resources), outtakes (intermediate changes), or outcomes (long-term impacts). 

Answer: B
→ truth=C  predicted=B  correct=False  tokens=90  latency=0.7s

Option C is a general statement about prevention, not a description of an event.

What should you recommend?
A. Quickly investigate to make certain your product is definitely the cause of the illness.
B. Stop all sales of the product throughout the nation and issue a recall for that product.
C. Stop sales of the product in the Albany, New York, market and issue a recall for that product.
D. Issue a press release to the Albany, New York, market explaining how to treat the symptoms.
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

Option A (investigate) is necessary but insufficient on its own—delaying action could worsen public trust and health risks. Option D (press release on treatment) is inadequate as it fails to address the root cause or prevent further harm. Option C (local recall) limits the response to Albany, but since the product is sold nationwide and the contamination may affect others, a broader action is needed.

Answer:

[public_relations] Q20  answer=B
Which communication model describes the encoding and decoding of messages?
A. Shannon and Weaver model
B. Osgood and Schramm model
C. Westley and MacLean model
D. Maletzke model
Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
The Shannon and Weaver model is the communication model that describes the encoding and decoding of messages. It was developed in 1948 and focuses on the transmission of information from a sender to a receiver through a channel, with the process involving encoding (converting the message into a signal) and decoding (reconstructing the message from the signal). This model is foundational in communication theory and emphasizes the technical aspects of communication, including noise and signal transmission.

Answer: A

Answer:
────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

In the context of substance abuse treatment, it typically involves rewarding individuals for negative drug test results, which indicates abstinence from drugs. This approach is not a last resort but a well-established method used to promote behavior change.

Option B is incorrect because it contradicts the principle of the intervention—patients are rewarded for negative (not positive) urine specimens. Option D is incorrect because the intervention targets drug use, not condom use among sex workers.

The correct answer is C: Paying sex workers to use condoms. However, this is not the standard definition of contingency management in substance abuse treatment. Re-evaluating the options, the most accurate answer based on the standard definition of contingency management in substance abuse treatment is rewarding drug users for negative urine specimens.

Therefore, the correct answer is:
